# 2627149

1   Combination of a crop model and a geochemical model 2   as a new approach to evaluate the sustainability of an 3               intensive agriculture system 4       Gihan Mohammed^1,, Fabienne Trolard^1,∗, Marina Gillon^1,, Anne-Laure 5              Cognard-Plancq^1,, André Chanzy^1,, Guilhem Bourrié1, [SOURCE: Page_1]

6   Abstract 7       By combining a crop model (STICS) and a geochemical model (PHREEQC), 8   a new approach to assess the sustainability of agrosystems is proposed. It is 9   based upon aqueous geochemistry and the stepwise modifications of soil so- Version postprint [SOURCE: Page_1]

10   lution during its transfer from the surface till aquifer. Meadows of Crau (SE 11   France), irrigated since the 16th century, were field monitored (2012-2015) 12   and modelled. Except for N, the mineral requirements of hay are largely 13   covered by dissolved elements brought by irrigation water with only slight 14   deficits in K and P, which are compensated by P-K fertilizers and the winter 15   pasture by sheep. N cycle results in a very small nitrate leakage. The main 16   determinants of the chemical composition changes of water are: concentra- 17   tion by evaporation, equilibration with soil pCO$_2$ , mineral nutrition of plants, 18   input of fertilizers, sheep grazing, mineral-solution interactions in superficial 19   formations till the aquifer, including ion exchange. Inverse modelling with 20   PHREEQC allows for quantifying these processes. For groundwater, mea- 21   sured composition fit statistically very well with those computed, validating 22   thus this approach. This long-term established agrosystem protects both soil 23   and water resources: soil nutritional status remains constant with even some 24   P and (minor) K fixation in soils; long-term decarbonatation occurs but it is 25   greatly slowed by saturation of irrigation water by carbonate; P fixation in 26   soil protects groundwater from eutrophication. 27   Keywords: Biogeochemical modelling, Mineral flux, Irrigation, Meadow, 28   Water quality [SOURCE: Page_1]

29   1. Introduction [SOURCE: Page_2]

30       In the circumstances of global changes, i.e. urban sprawl and climate 31   change, risks of degradation of natural and agricultural environments increase 32   and territories become more vulnerable (Pachauri et al., 2007; Zhang et al., 33   2007; Pageaud and Carré, 2009; Olioso et al., 2013; Baillieux et al., 2015; 34   Trolard et al., 2016). Climate change may positively or negatively impact 35   the services provided by ecosystems on which our social systems depend 36   (Trolard and Dangeard, 2014). 37       To ensure the resilience of a territory face to these changes, we must start Version postprint [SOURCE: Page_2]

38   from the concepts of limited space, scarce resources especially soil and water 39   and integrate information (Trolard et al., 2013b). 40       It is recognized that irrigated systems can regulate the local climate, water 41   resources and food security but little attention has been spent to the influence 42   of nutrients in irrigation water, except in the special case of the application of 43   treated wastewater for agricultural irrigation, which could provide nutrients 44   and provide economic benefits (Cromer et al., 1984; Burau et al., 1987; Asano, 45   1998; Shuval, 1991; Oron et al., 1995; Lubello et al., 2004; Bixio et al., 2006; 46   Tsiridis et al., 2009; Angelakis and Bontoux, 2001). Water scarcity and 47   increase of costs of fertilizers make a more rigorous evaluation of water use 48   in agriculture necessary. 49       Groundwater and surface water are not isolated. Thereby, any develop- 50   ment or contamination of one normally affects the other and the general com- 51   position of rivers (Livingstone, 1963; Sophocleous, 2002; Goren et al., 2014). [SOURCE: Page_2]

52   Irrigation water directly influences groundwater quality: nutrient fluxes were 53   paid a lot of attention to (Valett et al., 1996; Dahm et al., 1998; Bourg and 54   Bertin, 1993; Gibert et al., 1997; Sophocleous, 2002). 55       The processes at the interface between surface water and groundwater 56   affect solute retention in streams (Valett et al., 1996) and lateral nutrient 57   fluxes between uplands and aquatic ecosystems (Dahm et al., 1998; Wallis 58   et al., 1981; Grimm and Fisher, 1984). 59       In coastal areas, phreatic water exerts a pressure on sea water to pre- 60   vent salt intrusion and protects resource for drinking water, agricultural and industrial needs, including tourism (Mayer et al., 2016). Version postprint [SOURCE: Page_3]

61 [SOURCE: Page_3]

62       Other studies revealed that plant activity enriches the upper part of soils 63   with inorganic elements used during growth (e.g.Si, K, Ca, Mg) (Jobbágy 64   and Jackson, 2001; Lucas, 2001; Barré et al., 2009). Some studies have 65   provided evidence of plants accelerating silicate weathering and CO$_2$ con- 66   sumption (Alexandre et al., 1997; Kelly et al., 1998; Moulton et al., 2000; 67   Hinsinger et al., 2001; Song et al., 2011, 2012). 68       These studies point to the important role of plant to modify mineral 69   balance of soil solution. Nevertheless, accounting for plant uptake is rather 70   crude in biogeochemical modelling especially for daily uptake of inorganic 71   elements by plants (Hartman et al., 2007). 72       In this context, the aim of this paper is to assess the sustainability of an 73   intensive agricultural system under mediterranean to semi-arid climate. 74       The components of sustainability addressed here are: i) the short-term [SOURCE: Page_3]

75   nutritional status of soils ; ii) the long-term tendency of soils towards decar- 76   bonatation and acidification, and iii) the quality of phreatic groundwater as 77   a regional resource. Our approach combines a crop model (STICS) and a 78   geochemical model (PHREEQC) and is based upon aqueous geochemistry to 79   simulate the stepwise modifications of soil solution during its transfer from 80   the surface till aquifer. Though PHREEQC is basically a geochemical model, 81   it is here extended for the first time to simulation of uptake of elements by 82   plants, and use of proton balance allows for taking into account the rhizo- 83   sphere effect of acidification of soil solution. Version postprint [SOURCE: Page_4]

84   2. Modelling [SOURCE: Page_4]

85   2.1. State of the art on biogeochemical models [SOURCE: Page_4]

86       Different models combine geochemical processes in agroecosystems, as 87   illustrated in table 1. Most models have been developed for specific purposes: 88   e.g. N and C cycles, acid rain or stress effects on forests. 89       Heat, water, nitrogen (N) and carbon (C) fluxes are simulated in many 90   models of soil-plant-atmosphere system (Cramer et al., 2001; Morales et al., 91   2005; Yu et al., 2006; De Kauwe et al., 2013; Forster et al., 2013; Friedling- 92   stein et al., 2014; Walker et al., 2014; Wenzel et al., 2014; Sándor et al., 93   2016; Senapati et al., 2016; Zhang et al., 2016), but do not pay attention to 94   inorganic elements. 95       Nitrogen transport is studied through linking the hydrological flowpath 96   and biogeochemical pathway in saturated / near-stream zones: this shows [SOURCE: Page_4]

97   that surface / groundwater exchanges change rapidly on the timescale of 98   hydrologic events, which controls the fate of N (Cirmo and McDonnell, 1997). 99      Solute retention in streams and lateral nutrient fluxes are modelled by 100   linking hydrologic retention, biological nutrient cycling and chemical pro- 101   cesses at the surface water / groundwater interface (Valett et al., 1996; Dahm 102   et al., 1998). 103      Two models, PaSim and Biome-BGC MuSo, linking climate, soil, vege- 104   tation and management to ecosystem biogeochemical cycles were compared 105   to simulate C cycle (Sándor et al., 2016), and fluxes between the terrestrial biosphere and the atmosphere in the context of the global change (Xiao et al., Version postprint [SOURCE: Page_5]

106 [SOURCE: Page_5]

107   2012; Cramer et al., 1999; Jung et al., 2007; Huntzinger et al., 2012). 108      Soil chemical and biological properties are combined to assess how nutri- 109   ents cycling could be affected by wildfire (Fultz et al., 2016). The alpine hy- 110   drochemical model (AHM) gathered hydrological, geochemical, climatic and 111   biogeochemical data sets to simulate daily stream chemical composition and 112   its reaction to N deposition, but it cannot reflect terrestrial nutrient cycling 113   (Meixner et al., 2000). SOILVEG and the combined FORGRO/NUCSAM 114   gather biogeochemical and canopy processes to study effects of multiple stres- 115   sors on forest stands, but they do not simulate stream chemistry (van Heerden 116   et al., 1995; Mohren and van de Veen, 1995). 117      To capture ecosystem and surface water chemical response to atmospheric 118   deposition and climate, biogeochemical model is established to simulate soil 119   and surface water chemistry by linking ecosystem model (DayCent) with [SOURCE: Page_5]

120   geochemical model (PHREEQC), nevertheless this model simulates only C, 121   N, P, and S dynamics, there is no assimilation of cations (Ca$_2$+ , Mg$_2$+ , K+ ) 122   by plants (Hartman et al., 2007). 123      None of the preceding combination of models include both crop models 124   for grassland ecosystems and biogeochemical models with their influence on 125   soil solution and water quality. 126      Crop models can provide daily simulations of growth, but daily plant 127   chemical analyses are very complicated and expensive to obtain. [SOURCE: Page_6]

128   2.2. Biogeochemical processes Version postprint [SOURCE: Page_6]

129      Many processes influence the chemical composition of soil solution (Fig. 130   1): evapotranspiration, exchange of matter with soil and plants, inputs of 131   fertilizers and manure (Mohammed et al., 2016b) and ion exchange. Thus 132   it is necessary to acquire data on: i) the chemical composition of irrigation 133   water and groundwater; ii) the nature and quantities of fertilizers and manure 134   input on grassland soils; iii) the quantities of chemical elements harvested 135   and exported during the various cuts of hay; iv) the effect of winter grazing 136   meadows by sheeps, and v) pCO$_2$ and temperature conditions in the soil. 137      The approach is based on combination of existing models, namely the crop 138   model STICS, (Simulateur mulTIdisciplinaire pour les Cultures Standard) 139   (Brisson et al., 2008) and the geochemical model PHREEQC (pH-REdox- 140   EQuilibrium written in C language) (Parkhurst and Appelo, 2013) (Fig. 2). 141   This interface is a part included in the general PRECOS framework approach [SOURCE: Page_6]

142   (Trolard et al., 2016). Basic principles of both models are given below. 143      STICS is a crop model, such as CERES, PANORAMIX, SUNFLO, PASIM, 144   GRAECO (Brisson and Levrault, 2010). More specifically, STICS simulates 145   the behavior of the soil-crop system in daily time step within 1 year. The up- 146   per limit of the system is the atmosphere characterized by standard climatic 147   variables (solar radiation, minimum and maximum temperature, precipita- 148   tion, reference evapotranspiration) and the lower limit corresponds to the 149   soil/subsoil interface. STICS model is composed of seven modules: growth, 150   development, shoot and root growth, yield components, thermal environ- ment, water balance and nitrogen balance (Brisson et al., 2008). Leaf area Version postprint [SOURCE: Page_7]

151 [SOURCE: Page_7]

152   index is a dynamic parameter used to simulate radiative budget, taking into 153   account the morphology of plant cover. Carbon assimilation is simulated with 154   detailed biochemistry of photosynthesis and gross respiration. The effect of 155   CO$_2$ on stomatal resistance is taken into account to compute the budget of 156   radiative and convective energies. Water is transferred in soil to the underly- 157   ing horizon as soon as water holding capacity is exceeded. For organic matter 158   evolution, N cycle is first simulated, then C evolution is derived, by assuming 159   a constant value of C/N ratio (Brisson and Levrault, 2010). 160      STICS takes into account climate, soil characteristics, the type of crop 161   and crop management. Its outputs are the production (yield and quality of 162   biomass), and water and nitrogen balances. STICS was adapted for grassland 163   (Ruget et al., 1999) and tested with success for various types of grasslands 164   (Ruget et al., 2006). [SOURCE: Page_7]

7 [SOURCE: Page_7]

Science of the Total Environment, 595, 119-131. , DOI : 10.1016/j.scitotenv.2017.03.146 [SOURCE: Page_7]

165 [SOURCE: Page_8]

166      PHREEQC (Parkhurst and Appelo, 1999, 2013) is designed to perform a 167   wide variety of low-temperature aqueous geochemical calculations. PHREEQC 168   is based on an ion-association aqueous model and has capabilities for : (1) 169   speciation and saturation-index calculations; (2) batch-reaction and one- 170   dimensional (1D) transport calculations involving reversible reactions, which 171   include aqueous, mineral, gas, solid-solution, surface-complexation, and ion- 172   exchange equilibria, and irreversible reactions, which include specified mole 173   transfers of reactants, kinetically controlled reactions, mixing of solutions, and pressure and temperature changes; and (3) inverse modelling, which Version postprint [SOURCE: Page_8]

174 [SOURCE: Page_8]

175   finds sets of mineral and gas mass transfers that account for differences in 176   composition between waters, within specified compositional uncertainty lim- 177   its. It calculates the degree of saturation indexes (SI = log IAP −log K(T )), 178   where IAP is the Ionic Activity Product and K is the equilibrium constant 179   of the reaction at temperature T for various minerals (calcite, gypsum etc.), 180   changes in the water quality during its pathway through the soil, the amounts 181   of dissolved or precipitated minerals, ion exchange and surface complexation 182   phenomena. 183      In every case, PHREEQC can calculate changes in the chemical compo- 184   sition of the soil solution. PHREEQC takes thus into account the major 185   geochemical processes that determine the water quality in a “water - plant - 186   soil - atmosphere” system (WPSA). 187      PHREEQC has been combined with hydrological models to simulate pro- [SOURCE: Page_8]

8 [SOURCE: Page_8]

Science of the Total Environment, 595, 119-131. , DOI : 10.1016/j.scitotenv.2017.03.146 [SOURCE: Page_8]

188   cesses in deep aquifers, oil fields or geothermal energy production systems, 189   assessment of contaminants or elements transport in watershed and to assess 190   the hydrogeochemistry of seawater intrusion (Van Berk et al., 2009; Bozau 191   et al., 2015; Papassiopi et al., 2014; Korrani et al., 2015; Garneau et al., 192   2017; Boluda-Botella et al., 2014; Rezaei et al., 2005), simulation of dis- 193   solved organic carbon (DOC) degradation using first-order kinetics, assuming 194   two DOC fractions differing in reactivity (van Breukelen et al., 2004), and 195   for predicting and designing biodenitrification applications including in the 196   model microbiological processes - exogenous and endogenous nitrate respira- tion coupled to microbial growth and decay (Rodríguez-Escales et al., 2014). Version postprint [SOURCE: Page_9]

197 [SOURCE: Page_9]

198   Inverse modelling was used to explain the modifications of the geochemistry 199   of groundwaters of the three main aquifers of Sahara (Slimani et al., 2016). [SOURCE: Page_9]

200   2.3. STICS Outputs used to interface with PHREEQC [SOURCE: Page_9]

201         From climatic data, rainfall P , temperature t, irrigation I, and crop pa- 202   rameters, STICS calculates many parameters, of which a few are used here 203   to interface with PHREEQC, namely: (i) evapotranspiration ET , (ii) the 204   amount of water that drains below the root system into the deeper horizons 205   of soil (drainage D), (iii) growth and the amount of biomass produced (yield 206   R). 207         All these output data, measured during the same time interval, are used 208   to calculate the factor of water concentration by evaporation (Table 2) as : [SOURCE: Page_9]

9 [SOURCE: Page_9]

209      The P , I, R and D quantities provided by STICS allow for computing 210   fluxes (mass/unit area) from concentrations of elements in plants (mass of 211   element/kg of dry matter DM), in irrigation water, groundwater and soil 212   solution (mass/volume of soil solution). 213      The flux of inorganic element exported by crop (Qb ) is given by: Version postprint [SOURCE: Page_10]

Qb = R[E]b ,                                     (2) [SOURCE: Page_10]

214   where [E]b is the concentration of an element E in plant (biomass) (mol kg−1 215   DM), and R the yield of exportation (kg of DM m−2 ), and Qb is in mol m−2 . 216      The flux of inorganic element brought by irrigation water is given by: [SOURCE: Page_10]

Qp = P ρw [E]p + Iρw [E]I , [SOURCE: Page_10]

≃ Iρw [E]I ,                                        (3) [SOURCE: Page_10]

217   where [E]p is the concentration of an element in rainwater, [E]I is the con- 218   centration of an element in irrigation water (mol kg−1 of water), and ρw is 219   the specific mass of water (≃ 1 kg L−1 ). Rainwater is here considered as [SOURCE: Page_10]

220   contributing negligibly to the supply of elements to the system, except for a 221   slight contribution for N, taken as constant from STICS. 222      The flux of inorganic element exported by drainage (Qd ) is given by: [SOURCE: Page_11]

Qd = ρw D[E]d ,                                    (4) [SOURCE: Page_11]

223   where [E]d is the concentration of an element in soil solution (mol kg−1 of 224   water), D the drainage (mm = L m−2 of water), and Qd is in mol m−2 . 225      These equations are then used in PHREEQC simulation to calculate Version postprint [SOURCE: Page_11]

226   fluxes of elements between soil, plant and soil solution. Eventually they allow 227   for computing the modifications of soil solution during its transfer through 228   soil from its entry via the irrigation channel till groundwater. [SOURCE: Page_11]

229   2.4. Proton balance [SOURCE: Page_11]

230      Acid / base reactions influence pH by a variety of reactions: exchange of 231   CO$_2$ between solution, soil atmosphere and external atmosphere, calcite dis- 232   solution / precipitation, N cycle and through nutrients absorption by plants 233   and excretion of protons in the rhizosphere. The rules for proton balance 234   have been established long ago, more specifically following acid rain studies 235   (Breemen et al., 1983; Bourrié and Lelong, 1990). The conservative function 236   is alkalinity, which is a linear combination of concentrations of cations conju- 237   gate from strong bases and anions conjugate from strong acids (Stumm and 238   Morgan, 1970; Bourrié, 1976). This linearity is conserved when converting [SOURCE: Page_11]

11 [SOURCE: Page_11]

The balance is not zero for the topsoil if there are net fluxes either 257   of NH+ 4 or of NO$_3$ output to groundwater, which is expressed in the algebraic – [SOURCE: Page_13]

258   difference as proposed by Breemen et al. (1983). 259      The amount of absorbed charge by plants is positive as dominated by K+ 260   uptake. The plant compensates for this excess positive charge by the root 261   proton transmission to the soil : Version postprint [SOURCE: Page_13]

263   2.5. PHREEQC simulation [SOURCE: Page_13]

264   2.5.1. Processes simulated by PHREEQC in this paper [SOURCE: Page_13]

265      PHREEQC is used: [SOURCE: Page_13]

266     1. to compute saturation indexes SI of solutions with respect to minerals 267         that can dissolve or precipitate; [SOURCE: Page_13]

268     2. to simulate the modifications of the chemical composition of soil solu- 269         tion when it equilibrates with minerals, e.g. calcite and apatite and 270         including CO$_2$ , as CO$_2$(g) is formally treated as a mineral; [SOURCE: Page_13]

271     3. to simulate irreversible dissolution of P-K fertilizers; [SOURCE: Page_13]

13 [SOURCE: Page_13]

272     4. to simulate nitrification as an input of nitric acid into solution, accord- 273         ing to reaction 7, which is equivalent to nitric acid dissolution; [SOURCE: Page_14]

274     5. to simulate the removal of some constituents from the solution: water 275         in order to simulate evaporation, specific minerals in order to simulate 276         uptake of inorganic elements (P, K, Ca, Mg, Na, S) by plants, and 277         nitric acid to simulate nitrate absorption by plants; [SOURCE: Page_14]

278     6. to compute by inverse modelling the best combination of geochemical 279         processes that explain the transformations of the soil solution in the 280         subsoil below the root system to groundwater. Version postprint [SOURCE: Page_14]

281   2.5.2. Dissolution of fertilizers and uptake by plants [SOURCE: Page_14]

282      Dissolution of fertilizers and uptake by plants are respectively simulated 283   as an addition or a removal of the corresponding electrically neutral com- 284   ponent to soil solution, in the same way as evaporation is simulated with 285   a negative coefficient for water. To avoid transient negative concentrations, 286   fertilizer dissolution was simulated before absorption by plants. 287      Irreversible dissolution of fertilizers is simulated as dissolution of any min- 288   eral. The chemical composition of fertilizers is known and the mineral com- 289   ponents are derived from the chemical and mineralogical analyses of fertil- 290   izers and from the nature of industrial processes: (i) inorganic P fertilizers 291   consist of calcium di-hydrogen phosphate, Ca(H$_2$ PO$_4$ ) · 2 H$_2$ O, more soluble 292   than apatite and obtained by acid attack of apatite by sulfuric acid, with 293   gypsum CaSO$_4$ · 2 H$_2$ O, anhydrite CaSO$_4$ and bassanite CaSO$_4$ · 0.5H$_2$ O as [SOURCE: Page_14]

14 [SOURCE: Page_14]

294   by-products; (ii) K fertilizers mainly consist of arcanite K$_2$ SO$_4$ , and sylvite 295   KCl; (iii) N input from N cycle is simulated as an input of nitric acid HNO$_3$ . 296      The uptake of inorganic elements by plants is simulated here by PHREEQC 297   as follows: (i) P absorption by plants is simulated as the removal of calcium 298   di-hydrogen phosphate from the solution; (ii) S absorption by plants as the 299   removal of gypsum; (iii) calcium being absorbed in excess of the sum of P 300   and S, the remaining Ca absorption is simulated as CaO removal from the 301   solution ; (iv) Na, K and Mg absorption by plants are simulated as the re- 302   moval of Na$_2$ O, K$_2$ O and MgO from the solution; (v) N uptake is simulated as nitric acid removal from the solution. Version postprint [SOURCE: Page_15]

303 [SOURCE: Page_15]

304      Calcium di-hydrogen phosphate, arcanite, sylvite, Na$_2$ O, K$_2$ O, CaO (lime), 305   MgO (periclase) and nitric acid were introduced in PHREEQC database as 306   PHASES; gypsum is already present in the database. 307      Activity coefficients were computed with Debye-Hückel extended law, as 308   ionic strength is small enough (≃ 0.01 M). An example of calculation is 309   given as supplementary material in annex. [SOURCE: Page_15]

310   2.6. PHREEQC - STICS combination [SOURCE: Page_15]

311      The modifications of the chemical composition of soil solution from the 312   input in soil to its output to groundwater are simulated step by step. At 313   each step, the integration time is long enough for the change in storage in 314   soil (∆S) to be negligible compared to the fluxes that pass through it; as a 315   result soil solution is considered as a node (Bourrié and Lelong, 1990). In [SOURCE: Page_15]

316   the case study (see below), the time step for crop growth is 1.5 to 2 months, 317   which is the interval between two successive cuts. 318      Accordingly, the sum of fluxes entering solution (positive) is equal to the 319   sum of fluxes leaving the solution (negative): [SOURCE: Page_16]

Qp + Qw + Qb + Qd = 0,                                     (9) [SOURCE: Page_16]

320      where Qb is the flux exported to plants (biomass), Qd the flux exported 321   by drainage to the next node, Qp the input flux by irrigation water from the Version postprint [SOURCE: Page_16]

322   previous node and Qw the flux provided by fertilizers and / or mineral disso- 323   lution or precipitation. The boundary conditions fix the values of Qp , inputs 324   by irrigation and rain at the surface of soil and Qd , drainage to groundwater 325   below the root system, given by STICS. 326      The number of moles (n) of dissolved fertilizers per m^2 of irrigated meadow 327   and relative to 1 kg of water that drains is derived by the following equations: [SOURCE: Page_16]

Qw = νE n,                                       (10) [SOURCE: Page_16]

328   where Qw is the input flux by fertilizers, νE is the stoichiometric coefficient of 329   element E in the fertilizer and n is the number of moles of fertilizer dissolved 330   per m^2 . This assumes that fertilizers dissolve stoichiometrically. 331      The mass balance for any element E can be written as: [SOURCE: Page_16]

Iρw [E]I + νE n − R[E]b − Dρw [E]d = 0.                            (11) [SOURCE: Page_17]

332      We chose potassium to calibrate the model, supposing that there is no 333   net fixation of K+ in soil (ion exchange) during the interval of cuts (several 334   months), or that it is negligible, if any; the composition of fertilizers is then 335   conventionally related to K+ , so that νE = 1 for K+ . For K+ , eq. 11 becomes: Version postprint [SOURCE: Page_17]

Iρw [K+ ]I + n − R[K+ ]b − Dρw [K+ ]d = 0,                           (12) [SOURCE: Page_17]

336      from which n is derived as: [SOURCE: Page_17]

n = Dρw [K+ ]d − Iρw [K+ ]I + R[K+ ]b .                           (13) [SOURCE: Page_17]

342   3. Material and methods [SOURCE: Page_18]

343   3.1. Case study [SOURCE: Page_18]

344      The Crau area is the study case, that was monitored during four years 345   (2012-2015). Situated in Provence (South-East of France), its climate is 346   mediterranean with a rainfall average about 550 mm/yr. Since the 16th cen- 347   tury, water has been withdrawn from Durance River to this area, which 348   has gradually constituted a dense network of channels (about 80 km long), 349   hence enlarging irrigated area (Bonfillon, 2003). The Crau area is divided 350   into i) the “dry Crau”, which is occupied by a natural steppe (9,200 ha) and Version postprint [SOURCE: Page_18]

351   ii) the “wet Crau” that is an irrigated area, mostly occupied by permanent 352   grasslands (15,000 ha). Irrigation contributes to about 70-80% of the total 353   recharge of the aquifer (Bourrié et al., 2013; Olioso et al., 2013; Trolard et al., 354   2013a). Durance River deposited pebbles, which were cemented by calcium 355   carbonate to form a pedogenetic puddingstone. Soils developed on this pud- 356   dingstone are calcic luvisols (Bouteyre and Duclos, 1994), with a topsoil of 357   40 cm to 50 cm thick, containing a large content of pebbles (Colomb and 358   Roux, 1978). The topsoil was progressively enriched in fine fractions due to 359   silt input from the Durance River through irrigation period from April to 360   September yearly till the 16th century. 361      Fertilization consists only of supplying P$_2$ O$_5$ and K$_2$ O for first and second 362   cuts (Diehl and Denoy, 1942; Mohammed et al., 2016a). N input is mainly 363   due to symbiotic fixation by Legumes with some delayed inputs by manure. 364   Yield income is by three hay cuts per year, in May, June-July and August- [SOURCE: Page_18]

18 [SOURCE: Page_18]

Science of the Total Environment, 595, 119-131. , DOI : 10.1016/j.scitotenv.2017.03.146 [SOURCE: Page_18]

365   September respectively. In addition, a “fourth cut” is grazed directly by 366   sheep in autumn and during the winter, but with a low value of digestibility. 367   Thus the passage of sheep fertilizes grasslands with dung and urine, which 368   represent a bulk mass of about 15 tons/ha to 30 tons/ha (Hugues et al., 1952). 369   This area produces a high-value hay with a Certified Origin Product label 370   (in french AOP), which is exported all around the world. 371      However, irrigated grassland areas in this study area are expected to 372   decrease by 6 % to 11 % in 2030, because of land uses change, specially ur- 373   ban sprawl, which jeopardizes the sustainability of groundwater and perma- nent grasslands, and reduction of irrigation by about 30% due to allocation Version postprint [SOURCE: Page_19]

374 [SOURCE: Page_19]

375   changes of water to other activities such as urban and industrial areas (Tro- 376   lard et al., 2013a, 2016). [SOURCE: Page_19]

377   3.2. Crop model STICS operation [SOURCE: Page_19]

378      Simulations with STICS were made using its regional version where every 379   irrigated grass fields (≃ 7000 plots in the Crau area) were simulated within a 380   multisimulation framework. Climate data were given by a station located at 381   the domaine du Merle managed by the INRA-Agroclim unit. Soils charac- 382   teristics were derived from the Arles soil map (Bouteyre and Duclos, 1994), 383   and the irrigation history: irrigation dates back to the 16th century, and 384   extended steps by steps (Bonfillon, 2003). Due to the silt input by irrigation 385   water, thickness of Ap horizons increases with the age of irrigation (Trolard 386   et al., 2016). Grass irrigation is made by flooding. Irrigation schedule is gov- [SOURCE: Page_19]

387   erned by water turns between April to September, which are usually fixed 388   every 10 days with an average intake of 200 mm. To characterize the irri- 389   gation agenda and harvesting agenda for all fields, a stochastic model was 390   implemented based on observations made in farms. Among the outputs, the 391   harvested biomass R and the drained water D were used in the study. The 392   yield estimates computed with STICS model were calibrated and validated 393   on a set of 80 plots where data were collected and measured during four 394   years (2006–2009) (Trolard et al., 2013b). The maximum uncertainties on 395   parameters calculated by STICS model were 5% for irrigation quantity, 3% for biomass production and 10% for evapotranspiration. Version postprint [SOURCE: Page_20]

397   3.3. Data collection and analytical methods [SOURCE: Page_20]

398      Meteorological data from INRA (Agroclim, Avignon) and Meteo France 399   stations were used in STICS model (minimum and maximum temperatures, 400   precipitation, solar radiation, wind speed and evapotranspiration of refer- 401   ence) to compute the yield (R) and drainage (D), for 2012-2015. 402      The data used in PHREEQC is the result of sampling field campaigns 403   during four years (2012-2015): irrigation water, groundwater, chemical com- 404   position of hay, mineralogy and chemical composition of fertilizers, collection 405   of historical chronicles (chemical analysis of hay, groundwater quality, climate 406   data). 407      For waters, temperature, pH and electrical conductivity EC were mea- 408   sured in situ; samples were filtered at 0.45 µm in the field; analyses of K+ , [SOURCE: Page_20]

Chemical analyses of previous years were collected from the 425   Crau’s hay Committee (Comité du foin de Crau, 2014). Enquiries were made 426   for fertilizer management, which supplied information about the inputs of 427   fertilizers (see Supplemantary material). [SOURCE: Page_21]

431   coefficient R$_2$ was computed; it describes the data dispersion compared to the 432   prediction.Then the root mean square error (RM SE), that gives an overall 433   assessment of the difference and the mean bias error (M BE), that char- 434   acterizes the bias of the difference, were used. Paired t-test was used by 435   direct comparison between measured and simulated data using the null (H$_0$ 436   measured and simulated data are not significantly different) and alternative 437   (H$_1$ ) hypothesis. The Nash-Sutcliffe efficiency (EF ) was computed to de- 438   termine the relative magnitude of the residual variance (“noise”) compared 439   to the measured data variance (“information”) (Nash and Sutcliffe, 1970). It can range from −∞ to 1 and an efficiency of 1 (EF = 1) corresponds to a Version postprint [SOURCE: Page_22]

440 [SOURCE: Page_22]

441   perfect match between simulated and observed data. 442      Index of agreement (d) is used to overcome the insensitivity of EF and R$_2$ 443   to differences in the observed and predicted averages and variances (Legates 444   and McCabe, 1999); d is the ratio of the mean square error and the potential 445   error, its range is similar to that of R$_2$ and lies between 0 (no correlation) 446   and 1 (perfect fit). [SOURCE: Page_22]

447   4. Results and Discussion [SOURCE: Page_22]

448      The chemical composition of irrigation water (Tables A$_1$ to A$_5$ in Sup- 449   plementary material) shows only slight variations of Na+ , Cl– and SO$_2$– 4 ; [SOURCE: Page_22]

450   pH, Ca$_2$+ , alkalinity and Mg$_2$+ are quasi-constant. Phosphate, fluoride and 451   ammonium concentrations were smaller than the detection limits. Nitrate 452   concentrations are small, but non negligible, which implies there is some N- [SOURCE: Page_22]

453   leakage out of the system. Silica concentrations are small in between 2 and 454   5 mg SiO$_2$ kg−1 . 455      The chemical composition of groundwater shows even smaller variations; 456   pH is lower, while inorganic C and N concentrations are larger than in irri- 457   gation water (Table A$_6$ in Spuplementary material). The chemical composi- 458   tion of hay shows systematic variations: the contents of inorganic elements 459   increase from Cut^1 < Cut^2 < Cut^3 (Table A$_7$ in Supplementary material) 460   (Mohammed et al., 2016a). [SOURCE: Page_23]

461   4.1. Mineral elements balances Version postprint [SOURCE: Page_23]

462      STICS outputs, yield R, drainage D and factor of concentration of solu- 463   tion by evaporation fc are given in Table A$_8$ (Supplementary material), time 464   step considered is the interval between cuts, with three cuts per year. Using 465   equations 2 and 3, fluxes brought by irrigation water QI and exported by 466   plants Qb were computed. 467      Mass balance calculations show that irrigation water meets the plant min- 468   eral needs except for P and K (Table 3). [SOURCE: Page_23]

474      • log of equilibrating partial pressure of CO$_2$ for irrigation waters is 475         −3.25 ± 0.07 (n = 12), i.e. ≃ 0.056%, which is slightly larger than 476         atmospheric pCO$_2$ , ≃ 0.035%; [SOURCE: Page_24]

482      The implications are : (i) pH is buffered by solid / soil solution carbonate 483   equilibria, which limits the natural tendency of soils towards acidification; 484   (ii) large concentrations of calcium are present in solution and in soil; (iii) 485   water quality is good, and salinity hazard is very small. [SOURCE: Page_24]

494      • output to groundwater of P is null (ADES, 2016), whereas P inputs by 495         fertilizers are necessary to complete P brought by irrigation. Indeed, 496         farmers bring P-K fertlizers with a slight excess of P with respect to K; 497         as P concentration in groundwater is null, this implies that P in excess 498         precipitates in the subsoil. As the geochemical environment is close 499         to equilibrium with calcite, the most likely form of P precipitation is 500         hydroxyapatite. This prevents phosphate to be transferred to ground- 501         water, which would result in eutrophication as there is some nitrate 502         leakage. Version postprint [SOURCE: Page_25]

503      The simulations of soil solution are divided into four main steps repre- 504   sented by four nodes (S) in the graph. Firstly, at S$_1$ node (Fig. 4) irriga- 505   tion water concentrates by evaporation and equilibrates with soil atmosphere 506   pCO$_2$ = 2 × 10−2 bar. This last value is derived from groundwater SI com- 507   puted by PHREEQC: as observed above, log pCO$_2$ in phreatic groundwater 508   shows very limited variations around −1.7. We consider that irrigation water 509   saturates soil pore space and infiltrates quickly. This limitates CO$_2$ diffusion 510   to external atmosphere, and water stays in equilibrium with CO$_2$ - soil at- 511   mosphere from the topsoil till the aquifer. 512      Then, at S$_2$ and S$_3$ nodes, soil solution equilibria are computed after 513   dissolution of mineral fertilizers (N, P, K, Ca, S) and uptake of inorganic 514   elements by plants, respectively. Finally at node S$_4$, the soil solution drains 515   below the root zone and relaxes to equilibrium with calcite and hydroxyap- 516   atite. The work flow used for simulation in four steps is given in Fig. A$_5$ in [SOURCE: Page_25]

25 [SOURCE: Page_25]

517   Supplementary material. [SOURCE: Page_26]

518   4.3.2. Reference simulation: first cut 2015 [SOURCE: Page_26]

519      For exemple for the first cut in 2015, over these steps, graphs of Ca, 520   pH, inorganic C and N balances are detailed in fig. 4. The initial chemical 521   composition of irrigation water is 1.683 mmol kg−1 of Ca, 3.176 mmol kg−1 of 522   inorganic C and 0.025 mmol kg−1 of N, with pH = 8.45. 523      At S$_1$ node, the irrigation water is diluted by rain water and then con- 524   centrated by evaporation. These two opposed effects directly determine the 525   concentration factor (fc ) according to eq. 1 so that fc = 1.34. Version postprint [SOURCE: Page_26]

526      This leads to an increase of concentrations of Ca, inorganic C and N in wa- 527   ter by f c = 1.34 respectively to 2.255 mmol kg−1 for Ca, to 4.256 mmol kg−1 528   for inorganic C and to 0.034 mmol kg−1 for N. This leads to an increase of 529   alkalinity with the same multiplying factor. As pH is an increasing func- 530   tion of alkalinity, pH is thus expected to increase, and consequently calcite 531   is expected to precipitate. 532      However, simultaneously, during infiltration in the topsoil (Horizon A), 533   irrigation water equilibrates with pCO$_2$ of the soil atmosphere, which is much 534   larger than pCO$_2$ in external atmosphere due to organic matter oxidation in 535   the soil. Therefore, changes in the chemical composition of water are the 536   result of two opposite processes: [SOURCE: Page_26]

537      • concentration by evaporation of a saturated water tends to favor the 538         increase of pH (Harvie and Weare, 1980), since the alkalinity is positive, [SOURCE: Page_26]

26 [SOURCE: Page_26]

539         and to precipitate calcite; [SOURCE: Page_27]

540      • dissolution of CO$_2$ tends to lower the pH and dissolve calcite. [SOURCE: Page_27]

541   The simulation shows that this latter phenomenon dominates: a small amount 542   of CO$_2$ dissolves and provides 0.137 mmol of C and leads to calcite disso- 543   lution that provides 0.432 mmol of Ca. These modifications keep the soil 544   solution in equilibrium with calcite, although irrigation water was initially 545   over-saturated, and can be explained by the fact that the residual alkalinity 546   of calcite in irrigation water is negative (Al-Droubi et al., 1980). As a result pH decreases to 7.13. Version postprint [SOURCE: Page_27]

547 [SOURCE: Page_27]

548      Then this solution is brought back to 1 kg of water by identically mix- 549   ing the resulting solution with itself, in order to keep the mass reference 550   to 1 kg of water. Thereafter, the volume of water does not change and the 551   concentrations can be directly compared with groundwater composition. 552      Statistical Student test (P = 0.05, ν = 36, Table A$_9$ in Suplementary 553   material) shows that composition of soil solution at node 1 is not significantly 554   different from groundwater for Na+ , Cl– , SO$_2$– 4 , K and pH; it is significantly + [SOURCE: Page_27]

555   different for inorganic C, Mg$_2$+ and Ca$_2$+ and even more for NO–3 , as nitrate 556   concentration is ten times larger in groundwater as compared to irrigation 557   water. 558      This means that: (i) even before taking into account fertilizers dissolution 559   and uptake by plants, for most major elements, by equilibrating with soil at- 560   mosphere pCO$_2$ and concentrating by evaporation, soil solution becomes very [SOURCE: Page_27]

561   close to groundwater and (ii) that some significant, though minor, changes 562   occur during its transfer through soil and puddingstone. These changes are 563   then simulated to improve the fits. 564      Hereafter simulation of water quality divides into two steps, first inputs 565   of inorganic elements are added to the solution from fertilizers and manure 566   (node S$_2$), secondly elements are removed from the solution by plants (node 567   S$_3$). At S$_2$ node, dissolution of fertilizers adds 0.147 mmol Ca. The amount of 568   dissolved fertilizer was fitted on the concentration of K+ in groundwater, and 569   it was simply assumed that fertilizers dissolve proportionally to the average composition of the P-K fertilizer. This results in a slight excess of P addition, Version postprint [SOURCE: Page_28]

570 [SOURCE: Page_28]

571   as the ratio P/K in fertilizers added is larger than the ratio P/K in the plant 572   requirement. 573      At S$_3$ node, roots absorb 0.236 mmol Ca and other nutrients, excreting 574   H+ , which acidifies the solution: pH decreases to 6.7. Fertilizer application 575   quasi-exactly compensates nutrient requirements. 576      Finally at S$_4$ node, the drainage water before reaching the groundwater 577   equilibrates with respect to calcite, hydroxy-apatite and pCO$_2$ , as this was 578   the boundary conditions fixed : (i) calcite dissolution occurs, which adds 579   0.444 mmol Ca and C, and (ii) slight amount of P reaches the groundwa- 580   ter, which is simulated as precipitation as hydroxy-apatite (Table 4); the 581   simulation of apatite precipitation is quantified as an irreversible fixation 582   of 0.016 mmol Ca; (iii) degassing releases 0.636 mmol CO$_2$ , which leads to 583   increase pH to 7.11. [SOURCE: Page_28]

584   4.3.3. Results for second and third cuts [SOURCE: Page_29]

585      The results obtained for the second cut (Cut^2) are similar to the results 586   for the first cut (Cut^1), but this is not the case for the third cut (Cut^3) 587   (see graphs Fig. A$_1$ to A$_4$ in Suplementary material). The major difference 588   is that there is no addition of industrial fertilizers, whereas dissolved (P-K) 589   from grazing manure added in winter, becomes progressively available (Kang 590   and Freeman, 1999). [SOURCE: Page_29]

591   4.3.4. Graphs of mass balances and pH [SOURCE: Page_29]

592      Graphs of mass balances of Ca and inorganic C and of pH changes were Version postprint [SOURCE: Page_29]

593   drawn for the three cuts and four years (Fig. in supplementary materials). 594   They show the sequence of geochemical processes that modify the chemical 595   composition of water from irrigation water to groundwater. It appears that 596   the irrigated agricultural system has the side effect of buffering pH of soil 597   solution by bringing carbonate; moreover, both in the topsoil and in the 598   subsoil, calcite dissolves: pH is buffered by equilibrium with calcite in a 599   pH range from 7 to 8.3, depending on the partial pressure of CO$_2$ in soil. 600   Carbonate input by irrigation water amounts to 75% of the carbonate content 601   of groundwater. This has a positive effect in maintaining soil quality and 602   fertility, as it opposes to the general geochemical tendency to decarbonatation 603   in the north of Mediterranean sea, to acidification and degradation of clay 604   minerals and soil structure. 605      Irrigation can affect soil carbon storage by altering both soil organic car- [SOURCE: Page_29]

29 [SOURCE: Page_29]

606   bon (SOC) and soil inorganic carbon (SIC) cycling (Hannam et al., 2016). 607   Here, we only discuss soil inorganic carbon. 608      During the water quality transformation, which takes place in the upper 609   horizon of soil (Horizon A), irrigation water dissolves calcite and CO$_2$,g of 610   the soil atmosphere to maintain the soil solution at equilibrium with calcite, 611   although irrigation water was initially supersaturated with respect to calcite. 612   For 2015 Cut 1, to obtain 1 L of groundwater, irrigation water starts with 613   1.34 L, and therefore 1.37 mmol of CO$_2$ (g) of the soil atmosphere is trapped. 614   Then, the solution re-equilibrates with the calcite, since there is a stock of “active limestone” in soil and in the calcrete (locally termed “taparas”). Version postprint [SOURCE: Page_30]

615 [SOURCE: Page_30]

616   As a result, 0.444 mmol of calcite dissolve and 0.635 mmol of CO$_2$,g degas, 617   which returns to the soil atmosphere per 1 kg of drained water below the root 618   system. 619      The balance is that 0.735 mmol of CO$_2$,g per 1 kg of drainage water is 620   dissolved and transformed into HCO–3 anion and drained to the groundwater. 621   The soil solution therefore plays here locally the role of CO$_2$ sink. However, 622   this results from calcite dissolution: [SOURCE: Page_30]

CaCO$_3$ + CO$_2$ + H$_2$ O                Ca(HCO$_3$ )2 ,                   (14) [SOURCE: Page_30]

623      and calcite is expected to precipitate later, either in the subsoil or in the 624   sedimentary basin. Then the reverse reaction will occur, so that ultimately 625   the small amount of CO$_2$ trapped in soil will be released to atmosphere. The [SOURCE: Page_30]

626   net balance is thus zero, and this does not act globally as a carbon sink. 627   Only calcium originating from Ca-silicates weathering can trap CO$_2$ from 628   the atmosphere. [SOURCE: Page_31]

629   4.4. Simulated and measured data [SOURCE: Page_31]

630   4.4.1. Global fit [SOURCE: Page_31]

631      The excellent correlation (R$_2$ ≥ 0.956) obtained between measured and 632   simulated values of groundwater validates the proposed approach. This is 633   illustrated in Fig. 5 and Table in Annex, with the results of statistical tests 634   of goodness of fits. A further scrutiny of Fig. 5 shows that all elements Version postprint [SOURCE: Page_31]

635   are close to the 1:1 line. K cannot be used for validation, as it was used to 636   fit the number of moles of fertilizers dissolved and our model was fitted on 637   the concentration of K+ in groundwater. Na+ , Cl– , SO$_2$– 4 and inorganic C [SOURCE: Page_31]

638   do not show any appreciable deviation. Instead, Ca and Mg show a slight 639   but net deviation. The differences (Table 5) are nearly exactly opposite, 640   with [Ca$_2$+ ] being slightly underestimated (∆ = 0.237 mmol kg−1 ) and [Mg$_2$+ ] 641   being slightly overestimated, (∆ = 0.234 mmol kg−1 ). The discrepancy for 642   Ca could neither be solved by modifying pCO$_2$ in the simulation, nor by 643   allowing calcite to precipitate with some oversaturation degree. This would 644   lead to a poorer fit for inorganic C. It is thus likely that some Mg is fixed in 645   soil and some Ca released in the solution by ion exchange on clay minerals 646   or exchange sites of organic matter, which was not included in the direct 647   simulation above. [SOURCE: Page_31]

648   4.4.2. Inverse modelling [SOURCE: Page_32]

649      Inverse modelling was used to simulate the modification from node S$_3$, 650   just after plant uptake, to groundwater, by introducing ion exchange, and 651   letting all minerals, i.e. calcite, hydroxy-apatite and CO$_2$(g) free to dissolve or 652   precipitate, CO$_2$(g) being formally treated as a mineral. PHREEQC can com- 653   pute the amounts of dissolved or precipitated minerals that account better to 654   explain the transformation of soil solution at S$_3$ node into groundwater. The 655   results show that ion exchange contributes to Ca release (3 × 10−4 mol kg−1 656   of water), against Mg (−2 × 10−4 mol kg−1 of water), Na (−2 × 10−4 mol kg−1 of water) and quite negligible K (−5 × 10−6 mol kg−1 of water) fixation; some Version postprint [SOURCE: Page_32]

657 [SOURCE: Page_32]

658   CO$_2$(g) escapes and excess P precipitates as apatite. All fits are better than 659   10% agreement between groundwater simulated and observed (Table 5). [SOURCE: Page_32]

660   4.5. Sustainability [SOURCE: Page_32]

661      Due to its chemical composition, irrigation water supplies most of nutri- 662   ents necessary to hay growth. It is completed by some P, K fertilizers and N 663   input from manure and recycling of N from soil organic matter. A slight ex- 664   cess of P precipitates as hydroxy-apatite in the soil or subsoil. Ion exchange 665   fix some Mg and Na against some Ca release, with a quasi-negligible K fix- 666   ation, which justifies our hypothesis to fit the amount of fertilizers dissolved 667   on K output. 668      Globally, some calcite dissolves, which is the general tendency. It is slower 669   than it would be under rainwater input alone, as irrigation water originates [SOURCE: Page_32]

32 [SOURCE: Page_32]

670   from the Alps, where limestones are abundant. 671      The nutrient status of soils is thus preserved, with some enrichment in P 672   and stability of K status, in a context of possible decrease of the availability 673   of P and K resources at a global level. 674      Groundwater quality is preserved, with a small leakage of nitrate, but ca. 675   9 mg N Liter−1 , which is much smaller than the limit for drinking water. P 676   is retained in soil, which prevents from eutrophication. 677      The present agro-ecosystem preserves thus both soil and water resources, 678   in a region submitted to large pressures from urban sprawl, in the immediate vicinity of Marseilles, Arles and several cities. It dates back to the 16th Version postprint [SOURCE: Page_33]

679 [SOURCE: Page_33]

680   century and is the basis for many labelled productions (hay, lamb, wool...). 681   It appears thus as a sustainable and productive agro-ecosystem. [SOURCE: Page_33]

682   5. Conclusions [SOURCE: Page_33]

683      The combination of a crop model STICS and a geochemical model PHREEQC 684   allows for modelling and quantifying the changes in chemical composition of 685   water from the surface till the aquifer by including together data from agron- 686   omy, climatology, geochemistry and soil science. The approach consists to: 687   (i) compute step by step the successive modifications of the chemical com- 688   position of water, considered as a node from its input in the agrosystem till 689   the groundwater in the aquifer; (ii) introduce the plant uptake in the geo- 690   chemical model PHREEQC; (iii) compute the flux and the mass balance for 691   all chemical elements and parameters defining the water and soil quality of [SOURCE: Page_33]

33 [SOURCE: Page_33]

692   agrosystem, and (iv) use PHREEQC as inverse model to quantify geochem- 693   ical processes below the root zone. 694 [SOURCE: Page_34]

695      The proposed model was tested and validated on four years of cultivation 696   of hay and for each cut per year in Crau area. 697   Results show that the main determinants of the chemical changes of water 698   composition during its transfer through soil are: concentration by evapora- 699   tion, equilibration with pCO$_2$ of soil atmosphere, mineral uptake by plants, 700   input of fertilizers, grazing by sheep during the winter and mineral-solution interactions in superficial formations, including ion exchanges, till the aquifer. Version postprint [SOURCE: Page_34]

701 [SOURCE: Page_34]

702   Except for N, the mineral requirements of hay are largely covered by dis- 703   solved elements brought by irrigation water with only slight deficits in K 704   and P, which are compensated by P-K fertilizers and the pasture by sheep 705   during winter. For groundwater, measured composition fit statistically very 706   well with those computed, validating thus this approach. 707   This long-term established agrosystem protects both soil and water resources: 708   soil nutritional status remains constant with even some enrichment in P as 709   apatite precipitation in soil or subsoil, a net Mg fixation and a slight K 710   fixation (at least a stability) on exchange sites; long-term decarbonatation 711   occurs as is the rule north of Mediterranean sea and in temperate climate, but 712   it is largely slowed by the input of dissolved carbonate in irrigation water, 713   originating from Alps’s limestones; groundwater quality is preserved from 714   eutrophication by P fixation in soil due to the large concentration of Ca in [SOURCE: Page_34]

34 [SOURCE: Page_34]

715   solution and in soil. 716   Aqueous geochemistry of this agrosystem reveals that irrigation via Durance 717   River provides beneficial effects such as savings of fertilizers, protection of 718   soil minerals against natural tendency to acidification, groundwater recharge 719   and control of groundwater quality. In addition it contributes to production 720   of a high quality forage (COP, in french AOP). This long-terme crop system 721   established back to the 16th century can thus be considered as a remarkable 722   instance of ecologically intensive agriculture. [SOURCE: Page_35]

Blanc and Jarrige, for the 725   generous access to their grasslands, to François Charron, Deputy Director 726   of the experimental domain (Le Merle, SupAgro Montpellier) and to Mi- 727   lanka Babic, Fabrice Flamain and Ghislain Sevenier for their help with the 728   sampling and laboratory analysis. We sincerely thank Françoise Ruget and 729   Patrice Lecharpentier for their help in crop model simulations and Bruno 730   Lançon from ISTerre, Grenoble University for the acquisition of X-ray dia- 731   grams on fertilizers. The support of Climate KIC (PRECOS programm) is 732   gratefully acknowledged. Three anonymous reviewers are thanked for their- 733   useful comments and pertinent remarks on the content of the paper. They 734   contributed to improve the quality and legibility of this paper. Table 5: Comparison of simulated soil solution for steps S$_1$, S$_3$ and S$_4$ with groundwater, concentration in mmol/l. [SOURCE: Page_58]

Ca            Mg          Cl            K             Na        S           C       N Aquifer av.           3.443         0.473       0.640         0.033         0.639    1.225        5.403   0.625 Aquifer sd.           0.19          0.05        0.01          0.01          0.05     0.13         0.80    0.12 S$_1$                    2.718         0.774       0.648         0.031         0.995    1.122        5.939   0.079 S$_3$                    2.629         0.707       0.660         0.038         0.824    1.337        5.939   0.625 S$_4$                    3.206         0.707       0.660         0.038         0.824    1.337        5.616   0.625 Inverse model         3.633         0.473       0.660         0.033         0.639    1.337        5.508   0.625 [SOURCE: Page_58]

S$_1$ = soil solution after evaporation, equilibration with calcite and CO$_2$(g) ; S$_3$ = soil solution after fertilizers (N, P, K) dissolution and plant uptake; S$_4$ = soil solution after equilibration with calcite, hydroxy-apatite and CO$_2$(g) . Inverse model optimizes mass transfer to account for transformation of solution S$_3$ to the aquifer. [SOURCE: Page_58]
